Here, a cross-scale design strategy is introduced that combines microscopic control of static constitutive properties with spectral engineering through macroscopic architecture. Beyond shaping frequency-dependent responses, this strategy modifies the corresponding broadband performance bounds in passive systems by engineering the static parameters entering causal constraints. The concept is realized in granular activated carbon, where reversible sorption enhances equilibrium compressibility. Extrapolation of a multiscale model supported by low-frequency measurements to the zero-frequency limit yields an effective static bulk modulus approximately half the conventional nonsorbing isothermal reference value. This reduction substantially lowers the corresponding causal minimum-thickness bound for rigidly backed absorbers. A cascaded hierarchical composite architecture further redistributes absorption toward the target frequency band, achieving near-perfect absorption with a normal-incidence absorption coefficient above 0.9 across most of the 40--6400 Hz range. Together, static constitutive control and macroscopic spectral design enable ultrabroadband sound absorption beyond the conventional isothermal thickness benchmark without violating causality. This strategy offers a new approach to extending the attainable performance of metamaterials. △ Less

    Abstract: …and event timing. Four commercial SPAD modules were monitored in darkness for more than 920 h. In practical systems, some SPADs exhibit intrinsic step-like changes in dark-count rate (DCR), commonly referred to as random telegraph signal (RTS) noise. Similar RTS-like variations, however, can also arise from stray light, temperature-dependent threshold crossings, cable disturbances, electrical interference, and other readout artifacts. Distinguishing intrinsic RTS from these artifacts is important because they originate from different mechanisms and require different corrective actions. We present a self-referenced diagnostic that primarily uses the low-to-high afterpulse-yield ratio R, together with complementary checks based on DCR-state reproducibility, cross-channel behavior, and event timing. Four commercial SPAD modules were monitored in darkness for more than 920 h. Three exhibited intrinsic DCR switching of 44.59%, 19.33%, and 2.77%, with corresponding R values of $0.9996\pm0.0027$, $1.0050\pm0.0023$, and $1.0027\pm0.0043$, all consistent with unity. Controlled experiments reproduced temperature-related threshold crossings, light leakage, cable-contact artifacts, and artificial electrical injection. These artifacts produced distinct correlation and timing signatures: temperature-related threshold crossings gave intermediate pair-survival responses, cable disturbances and electrical injection generated sub-dead-time events, while light leakage produced simultaneous cross-channel switching and nonreproducible DCR levels. Combining R with orthogonal timing and DCR-based checks provides a practical self-referenced diagnostic framework for distinguishing intrinsic RTS from non-intrinsic artifacts using primarily time-tagged dark-count data. △ Less

    Authors: Lorenzo Di Giacomo, Daniele Pranzetti

    Abstract: …Charge Representation Theorems describing the canonical realization of diffeomorphism symmetries in the presence of field-dependent vector fields and Lagrangian anomalies, corresponding respectively to vanishing and integrable Noetherian flux. We show that, within an ambiguity-free covariant phase space formalism based on the Noetherian split, they instead arise as intrinsic quantities completely determined by the Lagrangian through the geometry of the associated covariant phase space. We prove two general Charge Representation Theorems describing the canonical realization of diffeomorphism symmetries in the presence of field-dependent vector fields and Lagrangian anomalies, corresponding respectively to vanishing and integrable Noetherian flux. In both cases, we derive explicit expressions for the associated central extensions and establish the corresponding cocycle identities. This provides a constructive algorithm for determining central charges directly from the Lagrangian formulation of the theory, without requiring an explicit computation of the charge algebra. As a non-trivial application, we consider asymptotically locally AdS$_3$ spacetimes with Penrose--Brown--Henneaux boundary conditions. We recover the corresponding centrally extended asymptotic symmetry algebra, providing an explicit realization of the Charge Representation Theorems. △ Less

    Prepare-and-measure quantum key distribution from a bound on information leakage

    Authors: Abhishek Mishra, Michele Masini, Maria Balanzó-Juandó, Armin Tavakoli, Stefano Pironio

    Abstract: …secret bit per key round. For noisy implementations, we compute key rates using semidefinite programming relaxations. Our best protocols tolerate visibilities down to 0.84, corresponding to an 8% quantum bit error rate, compared with the 11% threshold of standard device-dependent BB84. ▽ More Every quantum key distribution (QKD) protocol must limit the information available outside the users' laboratories. In device-independent QKD this is essentially the only assumption made on the devices. In a prepare-and-measure setting it cannot be imposed strictly, since the sender's device must emit a signal that depends on her private input. The natural replacement, and a minimal one, is a leakage bound: a bound on how much information about the input leaves the sender's laboratory, through the intended signal or through any side channel. We ask how far this unavoidable requirement can replace a detailed model of the source. We show that this single bound, quantified by the probability of guessing the input from everything the source emits, suffices to prove security against collective attacks. In our analysis, nothing is assumed about the precise form of the emitted states, their dimension, energy or purity, nor about the receiver's measurement device, beyond the standard requirement that it does not leak Bob's data. The standard B92 and BB84 protocols do not certify any key under this minimal leakage bound assumption. But closely related protocols can and, in the ideal limit, achieve one secret bit per key round. For noisy implementations, we compute key rates using semidefinite programming relaxations. Our best protocols tolerate visibilities down to 0.84, corresponding to an 8% quantum bit error rate, compared with the 11% threshold of standard device-dependent BB84. △ Less

Wall deformation over the cardiac cycle is sub-millimetre, rapid and spatially heterogeneous, and is superimposed on larger rigid-body motion. This work adapts a registration-based kinematic framework, originally developed for abdominal aortic aneurysms, to cerebral arteries imaged with 7 T 4D phase-contrast MRI. Deformable image registration between diastole and systole was used to measure wall-normal displacement over the cerebral artery network, primarily the Circle of Willis and connected communicating arteries. Following this, transverse centreline motion was removed to separate local wall deformation from rigid-body vessel motion before estimating circumferential strain. Across 17 datasets, the 99th percentile wall-normal displacement was 0.256 +/- 0.037 mm, 35 % of the acquired voxel dimension, and the 99th percentile circumferential strain was 8.67 +/- 2.40 %. Correction of circumferential strain to exclude rigid translation motion reduced the 99th percentile strain estimation in the example case from 28.1 % to 10.6 %, as a significant portion of the apparent wall displacement corresponds to bulk motion of the vessel. The pipeline produces continuous displacement and strain maps of the intracranial arterial network, although the values are sensitive to segmentation accuracy, resampling and registration parameterisation and require future ground-truth verification. △ Less

    Two types of hairy black holes in the (3+1)-dimensional $O(3)$ sigma-model

    Authors: Jutta Kunz, Aliaksei Mikhaliuk, Yakov Shnir

    Abstract: …potential. Our results show that it supports two qualitatively different types of asymptotically flat hairy black holes, distinguished by their connection to the corresponding solitonic branches and by the structure of their scalar hair; the type I black holes are connected to the vacuum branch and type II black holes are connected to regular scalaron-supported spinning solitons. We analyze their global charges, horizon properties, and dependence on the model parameters, comparing the solutions with Kerr black holes. For sufficiently weak gravitational coupling, the branch connectivity changes: the fundamental branches of types I and II reconnect to form one continuous family, while their corresponding second branches form another. We find that, in contrast to the vacuum-connected type I family, the scalaron-supported type II black holes extend to substantially larger horizon radii while preserving a nontrivial scalar configuration outside the horizon. △ Less

R2A bypasses fragile visual features by structuring successful training demonstrations into an offline spatiotemporal motion primitive graph. During inference, a query-dependent Graph Neural Network dynamically localizes the robot's ongoing execution within this graph, retrieving structurally compatible future motion primitives. These retrieved geometric priors are then seamlessly harmonized with the frozen VLA's predicted actions via a learned, dimension-wise gating mechanism, adaptively rectifying erroneous spatial trajectories while preserving the policy's local reactivity. Extensive experiments across three state-of-the-art VLA architectures, GR00T N1.7, $π_{0.5}$, and StableVLA, demonstrate that R2A consistently and significantly enhances robustness against diverse contextual perturbations. R2A achieves absolute success rate improvements of up to +12% in simulation benchmarks (LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin2.0-Plus) and +40% in real-world deployments, without requiring any finetuning of the base policies.     Thermodynamics and phase structure of rotating Hayward-AdS black holes

    Authors: Qi-Quan Li, Yu Zhang, Hoernisa Iminniyaz

    Abstract: …, the curve is 8-shaped, corresponding to a first-order small/large black hole transition. For $P_z<P<P_c$, the curve is C-shaped, corresponding to a zeroth-order transition. These results show that the 8-shaped/C-shaped phase structure characteristic of regular AdS black holes persists under rotation, indicating… ▽ More We use the modified Newman--Janis algorithm to obtain the rotating ``mother'' black hole solution of the rotating Hayward-AdS black hole. We further derive the fundamental thermodynamic quantities of the ``mother'' black hole in the full unconstrained phase space $(S,J,q,α,P)$, and impose the regularity condition $M_1=q^3/α$ to obtain the thermodynamic quantities of the rotating Hayward-AdS regular black hole. This order of operations gives a Hawking temperature identical to the surface gravity temperature and produces the correct response functions of the regular black hole. For $P<P_c$, the Hawking temperature $T_2$ possesses a local maximum and a local minimum, which divides the black hole into small, intermediate, and large black hole branches, and the $P$--$V$ isotherms display oscillatory behavior characteristic of a liquid/gas phase transition. The small and large black hole branches are locally stable, whereas the intermediate black hole branch has negative heat capacity and is locally unstable. Increasing the rotation parameter $a$, magnetic charge $q$, or pressure $P$ reduces the region of the intermediate branch. The $G_2$--$T_2$ diagram shows that the phase structure, in contrast to the standard swallowtail of the Reissner--Nordström-AdS and Kerr--Newman-AdS black holes, is 8-shaped or C-shaped. These two shapes are divided by the characteristic pressure $P_z$. For $P<P_z$, the curve is 8-shaped, corresponding to a first-order small/large black hole transition. For $P_z<P<P_c$, the curve is C-shaped, corresponding to a zeroth-order transition. These results show that the 8-shaped/C-shaped phase structure characteristic of regular AdS black holes persists under rotation, indicating that it stems from the restructuring of the thermodynamic phase space by the regularity condition rather than from spherical symmetry. △ Less

    Abstract: …mass. We study this behavior in a two-parameter twin experiment and regularize the variational objective. However, the expected Tikhonov term favors c… ▽ More Variational inference with normalizing flows provides a flexible posterior approximation for Bayesian model calibration, but struggles when the posterior has several modes separated by regions of low probability mass. We study this behavior in a two-parameter twin experiment and regularize the variational objective. Under Gaussian observation errors and a Gaussian prior, the corresponding minimization objective is the expected Tikhonov calibration objective minus the differential entropy of the approximating distribution. However, the expected Tikhonov term favors concentration near one of the modes, while entropy maximization does not guarantee multimodality. We introduce a regularization term based on Rényi divergence that encourages the approximate posterior to cover the prior support. In the connected bimodal case, a flow approximation represents both modes; in the case with well-separated modes, the flow and Gaussian mixture frequently miss one mode. The introduced regularization reduces the near-collapse fraction from 50% to 25%. While the regularization improves mode coverage in this experiment, it changes the Bayesian variational objective, introduces a trade-off in approximation accuracy, and does not provide a general solution to mode collapse. △ Less

Furthermore, the proposed method demonstrated robustness to handedness, successfully classifying EEG data across participants regardless of their dominant hand. These findings highlight the potential of EMD-based feature extraction for identifying music-related neural patterns and contribute to advancing research in Music Information Retrieval (MIR), affective neuroscience, and the broader understanding of the neural encoding of musical structure. △ Less

Furthermore, we show that the question of correspondence results in different levels of complexity in terms of designing and understanding transitions. Depending on whether correspondence is 1:1, 1:N, or N:1, visualization transformations may be direct and easy to understand, or they may require more elaborate transition constructs, implying increased cognitive effort for interpretation. Particularly, cases of 1:N and N:1 correspondence pose challenges for designing visualization transformations. △ Less

    Matrix Samplers and Quantum-Proof Extractors: Their Correspondence and Almost-Optimal Constructions

    Authors: Kuan Cheng, Ruiyang Wu

    Abstract: We establish an equivalence between matrix samplers and quantum-proof randomness extractors. The classical sampler--extractor equivalence is established by a seminal work of Zuckerman (Random Structures & Algorithms, 1997). Using this equivalence, we construct explicit strong matrix samplers with optimal randomness overhead and near-optimal sample complexity via constructing quantum-proof extrac… ▽ More We establish an equivalence between matrix samplers and quantum-proof randomness extractors. The classical sampler--extractor equivalence is established by a seminal work of Zuckerman (Random Structures & Algorithms, 1997). Using this equivalence, we construct explicit strong matrix samplers with optimal randomness overhead and near-optimal sample complexity via constructing quantum-proof extractors. Specifically for matrix dimension $D$, accuracy $\varepsilon$ and failure probability $δ\leq\varepsilon$, our matrix sampler uses $m+O_α(\log(D/δ))$ random bits and $O_α(\varepsilon^{-2-α}\log^{1+α}(D/δ))$ samples, for any constant $α>0$. Compared with the matrix sampler of Xun and Zuckerman (CCC 2025), our construction has improvements in two aspects. Under the same optimal random seed length, we can replace the $\operatorname{poly} D$ dependence in the number of samples by $O(\log^{1-γ} D)$. Under the same scale of sample size, we can remove their $\log\log D$ factor in the length of the random seed. Our underlying strong quantum-proof extractor has output length $n-O_α(a+\log(1/η))$ and seed length at most $(1+α)\log a+(2+α)\log(1/η)+O_α(1)$ for $n$-bit sources with conditional min-entropy $n-a$ and error $η$. We also obtain strong quantum-proof extractors for every constant entropy rate, for any error as small as $2^{-Ω(n)}$, having optimal seed length $O(\log(n/η))$ and output length $Ω(n)$, improving previous constructions. △ Less

For a distilled 4-step FLUX.1-dev model, \emph{ReSeed} requires only $0.00196\%$ additional trainable parameters relative to the base model and is trained on 8,000 prompt--noise pairs, while keeping the distilled student fully frozen and requiring no target images, perceptual losses, or adversarial losses. Strongly intensive observables remove the contribution of source-number fluctuations at second order. Here we extend the construction to all cumulant orders. For independent identical sources whose emission law does not depend on the source count, we build a normalized hierarchy of strongly intensive observables with $n$ new algebraically independent quantities at order $n$. Every observable is normalized to unity for statistically independent particles, vanishes when both window multiplicities are fixed, and remains finite at equal positive window means. We give explicit expressions through fourth order and an all-order recursion. Analytical particle production models and PYTHIA calculations for $pp$ collisions at $\sqrt{s}=0.9$ and 13 TeV demonstrate the sensitivity to window geometry, event selection, and connected multiparticle correlations. Fits constrained by the mean multiplicities and second-order observables provide conditional higher-order benchmarks. Deviations of these benchmarks from the PYTHIA results test the assumed correlation hierarchy, subject to the quality of the lower-order fit. Preliminary ALICE measurements at $\sqrt{s}=13$ TeV are used to illustrate the corresponding model-dependent scenarios. △ Less

    Authors: Daniele Lizzio Bosco, Jacopo Cossio, Simone Faro, Francesco Pio Marino, Gabriele Messina, Carla Piazza, Giuseppe Serra

    Abstract: …gates required by the corresponding SWAP-based construction. The resulting method subsumes optimal SWAP routing within… ▽ More Efficient quantum circuit compilation is challenging for quantum computers, where limited qubit connectivity can introduce substantial overhead in the implementation of logical operations. Recent AI-assisted synthesis methods have shown considerable potential for reducing circuit complexity, but their role in uncovering principles that can guide the design of new deterministic compilation algorithms remains largely unexplored. In this work, we investigate this perspective for the synthesis of quantum permutations into CNOT circuits under constrained qubit connectivity. Using AlphaCNOT as an exploratory tool, we identify a recurring decomposition for remote transpositions that improves on the construction based on adjacent SWAP routing. For two qubits at graph distance $d>1$, the discovered pattern yields a topology-legal implementation using at most $6d-4$ CNOT gates, compared to the $6d-3$ gates required by the corresponding SWAP-based construction. We formalize this observation through a constructive proof to define Global Transpositions, a deterministic synthesis method that searches for a minimum-cost decomposition of an entire permutation into topology-aware logical transpositions. The resulting method subsumes optimal SWAP routing within its synthesis model and provides certified upper bounds on the topology-constrained CNOT cost. Our experiments also reveal circuits that cannot be expressed as concatenations of independently synthesized transpositions. Instead, these circuits exploit intermediate linear transformations that temporarily leave the permutation subgroup and combine partial effects of multiple logical operations, a mechanism we call intermediate CNOT fusion. Our results show that AI-assisted synthesis can support not only circuit optimization, but also the discovery of reusable identities, provable constructions, and hidden mechanisms in quantum circuit compilation. △ Less

    Residual dynamics determine dissipation in strongly coupled linear Langevin systems

    Authors: Vansh Kharbanda, Anton F. Burnet, Benedikt Sabass

    Abstract: …two-dimensional systems, this suppression is monotonic for nonzero coupling modes and guarantees a monotonic decrease of the entropy production rate (EPR), with the corresponding probability currents vanishing in the strong-coupling limit. In higher dimensions, suppressing the circulation associated with the coupled degrees of freedom does not generally elim… ▽ More Interactions shape the properties of many physical and biological nonequilibrium systems, but whether collective operation raises or lowers entropy production remains difficult to predict. Here, we show that strong coupling suppresses irreversible circulation associated with the directly coupled degrees of freedom. In identical two-dimensional systems, this suppression is monotonic for nonzero coupling modes and guarantees a monotonic decrease of the entropy production rate (EPR), with the corresponding probability currents vanishing in the strong-coupling limit. In higher dimensions, suppressing the circulation associated with the coupled degrees of freedom does not generally eliminate all irreversible circulation, since the remaining degrees of freedom can still sustain nonequilibrium currents. The EPR of these residual dynamics sets the strong-coupling limit, providing a criterion for whether a given choice of coupled variables leads to lower dissipation than the uncoupled system. The approach to this limit depends on how the finite-coupling current correction aligns with the residual current. Further, we show that convergence to residual dynamics in the strong-coupling limit also applies to coupled non-identical systems. Finally, we demonstrate the predicted EPR reduction in nonlinear models of vertebrate hair-cell bundles and bacterial chemotaxis. △ Less

Together, these observations relate deposition conditions to contrasting chemical and morphological starting states without establishing bulk stoichiometry or phase purity. A literature-grounded outlook identifies ion storage, sensing, electrochromism, resistive switching, and ion handling as research opportunities requiring functional validation in these films. △ Less

    Abstract Lipschitz Continuity

    Authors: Marco Campion, Isabella Mastroeni, Michele Pasqua, Caterina Urban

    Abstract: …in particular of program semantics, requiring that bounded differences between properties of two inputs yield proportionally bounded differences between properties of the corresponding outputs. ALC explicitly discerns between two complementary forms of approximation: property-based (or qualitative) approximations, which retain a property of the data and are… ▽ More We introduce Abstract Lipschitz Continuity (ALC), an extensional property of functions, in particular of program semantics, requiring that bounded differences between properties of two inputs yield proportionally bounded differences between properties of the corresponding outputs. ALC explicitly discerns between two complementary forms of approximation: property-based (or qualitative) approximations, which retain a property of the data and are formalized by upper closure operators as in abstract interpretation, and quantitative approximations, which measure the closeness of data and are formalized by pre-metrics, a weakening of metrics that covers the distances arising in program analysis. Keeping the two forms of approximation explicit has two main benefits. First, it allows us to precisely relate ALC to established extensional properties: ALC is a sufficient condition for partial completeness in abstract interpretation, for partial abstract non-interference in language-based security and, with a null Lipschitz constant, for abstract robustness in machine learning. Second, it supports reasoning about ALC of program semantics by induction on the syntax of programs: we present a sound deductive system, parametric in the property-based approximations and in the pre-metric of interest, that reduces a proof of ALC for a program to proofs for its basic commands and to some explicit side conditions, which isolate what makes ALC compositional across sequential composition, non-deterministic choice and iteration. △ Less

Although these calculations reduce the differences between the GF and BK predictions, they still lead to a much stronger onset of leading-twist shadowing effects compared to the GF formalism.     Maximum Energy of Integral Circulant Graphs with Same-Parity Exponents

    Authors: Jianwei Jiang, Chunhua Yang

    Abstract: …p and q are distinct primes at least five and a and b, both positive integers, have the same parity. Among all nonempty sets D of proper divisors of n, the energy of the corresponding integral circulant graph is maximised by exactly two divisor sets: the divisors p to the power i times q to the power j with i plus j odd, and the divisors with i plus j even a… ▽ More The energy of a finite graph is the sum of the absolute values of the eigenvalues of its adjacency matrix. Let n be p to the power a times q to the power b, where p and q are distinct primes at least five and a and b, both positive integers, have the same parity. Among all nonempty sets D of proper divisors of n, the energy of the corresponding integral circulant graph is maximised by exactly two divisor sets: the divisors p to the power i times q to the power j with i plus j odd, and the divisors with i plus j even after removing p to the power a times q to the power b. We also determine the exact maximum energy. For the proof, weighted prime-power Ramanujan transforms yield an exact tensor-product representation of the graph energy, reducing the extremal problem to a weighted cut separating two prescribed vertices. After a sign conjugation, the relevant transforms decompose into embedded path blocks; the nontrivial blocks correspond to a weighted path together with chords of length three of weight negative one. We prove a sharp lower bound for cuts separating the two prescribed vertices in Kronecker products of these path blocks. For products of two such nontrivial blocks, equality holds only for cuts that isolate one of the two prescribed vertices. Combined with the energy-to-cut correspondence, these equality cases yield exactly the two maximising divisor sets. △ Less

Successful manipulation depends primarily on how the robot and task-relevant objects evolve, whereas future-video prediction must also reconstruct the appearance and background of the entire scene. We therefore propose Trace-WAM, which augments video-action learning with future 3D motion prediction as an auxiliary objective. This objective supervises temporal point correspondence together with image-plane displacement and depth change, directly describing the interaction dynamics and spatial changes involved in manipulation. Because the action expert does not consume predicted trajectories, inference retains the current-observation policy path: a single visual forward pass followed by action denoising.     Kinetic Sectors and Nonequilibrium Dynamics in the $δ$-Ising Model

    Authors: Dehen Cao, Wei Jiang, Ya Zhang

    Abstract: …. Parameter sweeps correspond to ordered products of sector operators, which separate reaching a state from holding it, so hysteresis appears when a state is reached and released at different sector boundaries. We also show how increasing the system size can induce a Mpemba-like reversal, allowing a state with more misaligned spins to reach the final ordered… ▽ More We introduce and analyze the $δ$-Ising model, a kinetic Ising ferromagnet in which single-spin flips are accepted when their energy change does not exceed a tunable threshold. Varying the field and threshold opens or closes local flip channels, dividing parameter space into kinetic sectors, each governed by a fixed Markov transition operator. We prove that the dynamics retains global spin-inversion symmetry exactly when up and down spins have equal numbers of open channels. In one such sector on a periodic chain of length $L$, unbiased random initial states therefore reach the field-aligned and antifield states with probability $1/2$ each. Closing a single channel leaves both states absorbing but makes the probability of ordering against the field decay as $(2/π)^L$. Parameter sweeps correspond to ordered products of sector operators, which separate reaching a state from holding it, so hysteresis appears when a state is reached and released at different sector boundaries. We also show how increasing the system size can induce a Mpemba-like reversal, allowing a state with more misaligned spins to reach the final ordered state sooner on average. On a periodic chain, this reversal arises because the mean absorption time grows logarithmically with system size for many short domains but linearly for a single macroscopic domain, and it disappears when the bulk flip channel is opened. These findings show how a simple, intuitive rule for Ising dynamics links local kinetic constraints to field response, memory, and relaxation within a unified framework. △ Less

    Weakness Localization Is Not Enough: State-Aware Recursive Knowledge Improvement for Large Language Models

    Authors: Runze Jiang, Yan Wang, Boran Jin, Bingyu Zhu, Longtao Huang

    Abstract: …rounds. At each round, RKI localizes knowledge weaknesses, estimates the model's operational knowledge state from diagnostic evidence and intervention history, selects a corresponding intervention, and re-evaluates the updated model before determining the next intervention. On HotpotEval and SafeInternalEval, RKI outperforms existing knowledge incorporat… ▽ More Knowledge improvement in large language models is shifting from predefined knowledge incorporation pipelines toward evaluation-guided updating. Existing methods use evaluation to localize model-specific knowledge weaknesses and guide targeted post-training. However, identifying what to improve does not necessarily provide sufficient information for deciding how to improve it. The same localized weakness can be compatible with distinct operational knowledge states: the relevant knowledge may be unavailable, available but difficult to access reliably, or accessible but ineffective for downstream use. We call this ambiguity knowledge-state aliasing. When compatible states favor different interventions, greater diagnostic resolution alone is insufficient for intervention selection. Moreover, model updates can change the underlying knowledge state before the observed weakness disappears, such that an intervention appropriate in one round may no longer address the bottleneck in the next. To address these challenges, we propose Recursive Knowledge Improvement (RKI), which formulates knowledge improvement as a sequential, state-aware intervention process. RKI organizes knowledge into graph-structured units that provide a shared basis for diagnostic evaluation and targeted intervention across rounds. At each round, RKI localizes knowledge weaknesses, estimates the model's operational knowledge state from diagnostic evidence and intervention history, selects a corresponding intervention, and re-evaluates the updated model before determining the next intervention. On HotpotEval and SafeInternalEval, RKI outperforms existing knowledge incorporation methods by 18.41 points on average. Further analyses show that knowledge states provide additional guidance for intervention selection beyond weakness localization and can change across model updates even when the observed weakness persists. △ Less

This architecture mechanically decouples functional DOF to reduce unintended inter-joint motion propagation, while allowing independently addressable DOF to be actively coordinated into coupled multi-joint motion patterns during complex manipulation. Comprehensive experiments assess reachable workspace, coordinated fingertip trajectories, thumb opposition, 33-grasp taxonomy coverage, grasping force, payload capacity and task-level manipulation. We characterize the Green's functions of all strictly positive real self-adjoint realizations of this operator. The kernels are in one-to-one correspondence with positive-semidefinite $2\times2$ endpoint covariance matrices. We call the associated centered Gaussian processes Brownian processes. Each is a standard Brownian bridge plus the affine interpolation of an independent Gaussian endpoint vector. Singular endpoint covariance matrices give hard endpoint constraints, such as the pinned start $X(0)=0$ of Brownian motion. This parametrization gives explicit operator domains and energy spaces. On every mesh it gives an exact sparse precision matrix, so the likelihood of $n$ observations can be evaluated in $O(n)$ operations. Non-pixel-aligned methods address this limitation by predicting a fixed number of Gaussians. Without explicit ray and depth constraints, these methods yield misplaced Gaussian centers, causing rendering artifacts under large viewpoint changes. To address this issue, we introduce GeoGSplat, a geometry-grounded feed-forward model that explicitly incorporates geometric priors into the non-pixel-aligned framework. A dual-branch encoder constructs Anchors and Triplane to capture coarse 3D structure and fine-grained scene features, respectively. Subsequently, a hierarchical decoder progressively decodes dense 3D points and Gaussian primitives, enabling 3D reconstruction and novel view synthesis. GeoGSplat surpasses both state-of-the-art pixel-aligned and non-pixel-aligned Gaussian Splatting methods on DTU and NRGBD in terms of novel view synthesis and reconstruction. The advantage of our geometry-grounded paradigm further increases as target viewpoints move farther from the input cameras, demonstrating stronger generalization to large viewpoint changes. Project Page: https://comedy0913.github.io/projects/geogsplat △ Less

Across both species and recording m… ▽ More Individual neurons exhibit diverse molecular, morphological, and electrophysiological properties that shape their distinct roles in neural circuits. While these multimodal features are largely shared across individuals, they remain an underutilized source of information in analyzing neural population dynamics. We posit that these shared biological profiles provide a common reference for relating neural dynamics across subjects and recording sessions. Our framework leverages this structure by routing neurons into latent groups based on their biological profiles and analyzing population dynamics through group-level representations. Because these assignments are anchored in biological properties that are consistent across subjects, the learned organization supports transfer to unseen animals without retraining or neuron-wise correspondence. Across both species and recording modalities, the framework delivers state-of-the-art performance in both neural decoding and manifold analysis: it achieves superior decoding of behavior and stimulus labels while better preserving the temporal consistency and local geometric structure of latent trajectories on unseen data. Our experiments demonstrate that not only does providing biological features improve baseline performance, but our proposed framework achieves even stronger results by using these properties to explicitly structure the population rather than treating them as auxiliary inputs. Together, these results suggest that organizing neural populations by their multimodal biological features offers a promising framework for generalizing population dynamics across subjects and recording sessions. △ Less

    The Wess-Zumino Action of the Conformal Anomaly

    Authors: Emil Mottola

    Abstract: …corresponds to a cohomologically trivial cocycle, it also arises from the IR limit of massless quantum fields, independently of UV physics, and contributes to the WZ action for low energy gravity. Analogous to the WZ action for pseudo-Goldstone mesons in low energy QCD from the axial anomaly, the WZ action for the conformal anomaly provides a Nambu-Goldston… ▽ More The complete Wess-Zumino (WZ) action and stress-energy tensor (SET) of the conformal anomaly in both two and four dimensions is given. This local effective action is expressed in terms of a single scalar Nambu-Goldstone `conformalon' field $\varphi$, parametrizing the local abelian Weyl group of conformal transformations that act transitively on the spacetime metric. Satisfying WZ consistency, and equipped with a variational principle for $\varphi$ that determines the conformal transformation $g_{μν}\!\to\!g_{μν}e^{-\varphi}$ to an associated spacetime with no anomaly, the WZ action resolves the conformal gauge fixing ambiguity of earlier methods of `integrating the anomaly.' In $D\!=\!2$ the WZ action is the local form of the Polyakov action, whose massless $\varphi$ propagator describes a correlated two-particle scalar intermediate state in the SET polarization function $\langle TT\rangle$. The imaginary part of this polarization obeys a UV finite sum rule, becoming a Dirac $δ$-function at zero energy for conformal quantum fields, demonstrating the infrared origin of the anomaly, while the real part becomes the light cone propagator of $\varphi$. Although the $\Box R$ term in $D\!=\!4$ corresponds to a cohomologically trivial cocycle, it also arises from the IR limit of massless quantum fields, independently of UV physics, and contributes to the WZ action for low energy gravity. Analogous to the WZ action for pseudo-Goldstone mesons in low energy QCD from the axial anomaly, the WZ action for the conformal anomaly provides a Nambu-Goldstone scalar field for macroscopic gravity, in addition to the metric of classical general relativity. △ Less

The APS studies the interaction between structures of interest in Probability Theory such as Gibbs distributions, equations with noise, etc. and the axioms of the corresponding objects and transformations. We also introduce in Section 6 Transformations-Axiomatic Based Learning (TABLe) on Mathematical data-sets. One feature of this approach is the ranking of transformations from a table for a certain task based on scores which reflect which transformations work better than others, similarly to when human investigators try various methods on a task. We also study structure recovery from noisy data. In particular, to exemplify the ideas, we consider loss functions which contain derivations and derivations-like transformations. As a first application we study recovery in the context of polynomials with integer coefficients using standard derivatives along with other types of derivations which appear in Arithmetic: the p-derivations which satisfy \textit{different axioms} from the usual derivatives. As a first application of the APS we study a class of noisy (partial/mixed) derivations equations. In the last sections, to provide further potential applications of this framework, we give an overview of an initial simplified Opinion Dynamics model and its (simultaneous) study of rules of updates. △ Less

    Optimizing Evolution Under Cyclic Selection: the Case of B Cells

    Authors: Xingjian Hou, Colin Scheibner, Ned S. Wingreen

    Abstract: …selection events affects adaptation, we develop a minimal computational model of cyclic selection. We find that the optimal average mutation rate per division and the corresponding adaptation velocity are nearly invariant across number of divisions between selections, and we quantitatively explain this behavior with a theory based on the dynamics of the "… ▽ More Rapid evolutionary adaptation may be necessary for organisms facing strong environmental pressures. Although selection often acts continuously, in the case of B cells evolving high affinity antibodies in germinal centers, selection can take place after multiple rounds of division. To study how the number of divisions between selection events affects adaptation, we develop a minimal computational model of cyclic selection. We find that the optimal average mutation rate per division and the corresponding adaptation velocity are nearly invariant across number of divisions between selections, and we quantitatively explain this behavior with a theory based on the dynamics of the "nose", i.e. the fittest individuals in the population. We also find that when the number of divisions between selection events is large, then mutating every division results in slower adaptation than mutating only at the last division before selection (as is the case for B cells). Our model provides a framework for understanding adaptation in natural and experimental systems subject to cyclic selection. △ Less

Specifically, we show that any algorithm that outputs an $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation to maximum matching requires $Ω(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ passes for constant $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$. Prior to our result, such tradeoffs were proven only conditioned on a certain combinatorial assumption about the density of Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The state-of-the-art unconditional lower bound rules out any approximation factor better than $(8/9)$ in two passes but leaves open the possibility of a $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation for any $\varepsilon > 0$ even in three passes. We provide two different proofs of our result: the first proof uses the recent hierarchical-embedding framework of Assadi, Konrad, Naidu, and Sundaresan (STOC 2024) along with combinatorial constructions using Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The second extends the two-level Ruzsa-Szemerédi hierarchy of Konrad and Naidu (SODA 2024) beyond two levels and proves the corresponding communication lower bound directly by induction, rather than through a reduction to Index. △ Less

We report the serendipitous discovery of a giant ORC, ORC~J1928$-$5056, in 856~MHz continuum data from the ASKAP observations. The emission forms a single, mildly elliptical structure $4^{\prime}$ across, corresponding to $0.63$~Mpc at the redshift $z=0.1507$ of the central galaxy LEDA~140850, the brightest cluster galaxy of Abell~3639, which makes it one of the largest single ORCs known. After subtracting the radio nucleus associated with the host galaxy, the ORC has a flux density of 73~mJy integrated within an area of $\sim 12$~arcmin$^2$, i.e.\ a mean surface brightness of only $1.7$~$μ$Jy~arcsec$^{-2}$, and a rest-frame luminosity of $L_{1.4}=2\times10^{24}$~W~Hz$^{-1}$, comparable to those of the most luminous ORCs. Including its nucleus, the system reaches $L_{1.4}=6.6\times10^{24}$~W~Hz$^{-1}$, higher than that of any other ORC reported so far. The host is a massive ($M_{\ast}\sim10^{11}\,M_{\odot}$), passive elliptical galaxy with negligible star formation. It contains an AGN jet with a power-law spectrum of index $α=-0.877\pm0.089$ between 856 and 1656~MHz. Finally, we discuss the implications for ORC formation models and the observations needed to establish the nature of this system. △ Less

    Stationary Action Principles for Neuronal Growth

    Authors: Nathan Brodeur, Cristian Staii

    Abstract: …deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction gene… ▽ More Neuronal growth is guided by biochemical, mechanical, and geometrical cues while being influenced by stochastic fluctuations in cytoskeletal dynamics, adhesion, and growth cone sensing. Here, we develop two complementary variational descriptions of mechanically guided axonal growth. First, for neurons growing on periodic parallel micropatterns, we use Langevin and Fokker--Planck models to derive a stochastic path action incorporating deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction generates mechanical guidance and axonal turning at stiffness interfaces. In the quasi-static limit, this response leads to a Fermat-like spatial action with an effective axonal refractive index determined by the traction and substrate stiffness relationship. The resulting framework predicts axonal refraction across stiffness boundaries, including an approximate Snell-like relation and a critical angle condition. Although the stochastic and Fermat-like actions have distinct physical meanings and ranges of validity, both describe how substrate mechanics constrain axonal trajectories. These results connect measurable quantities, including drift, diffusion, stiffness, traction, and growth direction, to global properties of axonal paths and provide a framework for designing biomimetic substrates for controlled neuronal growth and regeneration. △ Less

It is demonstrated that introducing a dimensionless {\it WKB correction} $γ$ into the r.h.s. of the proposed B-S type quantization condition allows us to reproduce the exact energies with $γ$ being very small for positive degrees $m \geq 0$ and sufficiently small for non-positive ones $m < 0$ for all $n_r, \ell$ studied. △ Less

We consider matter composed of electrically neutral $ddu$ baryons, including neutron- and $Δ^0$-like states. Using a simple constituent-quark model, we compute the baryon wave functions and spectra in magnetic fields. The magnetic field polarizes the spin-flavor structure, compresses the internal wave function in the transverse direction, and strongly suppresses the transverse kinetic energy. We then use these distributions to evaluate quark occupation probabilities in an ideal baryon gas and determine the onset of quark saturation. At finite magnetic field in the $+z$-direction, the $d_\downarrow$ quark in the lowest Landau level reaches saturation first. The resulting saturation density depends nonmonotonically on the magnetic-field strength: it decreases at weak fields and increases at stronger fields. This behavior reflects the competition between spin-flavor polarization, which promotes saturation, and transverse spatial squeezing, which delays saturation by broadening of the quark transverse momentum distribution. △ Less

One important feature of BST is that the competition between the ferroelectric BaTiO3 and the paraelectric SrTiO3 parent phases can be tuned by the A-site alloy concentration, opening a platform for exploring dielectric phase competition. However, the understanding of the interplay between and establishing the control of the dielectric phase from thin film deposition parameters has not been sufficiently reported in the literature. Here, we synthesized a series of SrRuO3/BST/SrRuO3 epitaxial thin film heterostructures by Pulsed Laser Deposition (PLD) from a (Ba0.9Sr0.1)TiO3 target under systematic variation of the oxygen partial pressure during the BST deposition. We mapped the dielectric phase of the film to the oxygen growth pressure, film composition, and film crystal structure. A ferroelectric, relaxor-like, or paraelectric phase can be obtained with relatively moderate change in oxygen growth pressure. Scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) analysis further indicates that this pressure-dependent phase evolution is accompanied by a structural transition of the BST lattice from tetragonal toward a nearly cubic phase, with the latter corresponding to the relaxor-like state. The control of the dielectric phase opens a pathway to study phase evolution and enable future computing technologies. △ Less

    SG^2SLAM: Spatial Geometry-Informed 3D Gaussian Splatting SLAM for Monocular Outdoor Navigation

    Authors: Ziang Guo, Didar Seyidov, Yixiao Zhou, Xuefeng Zhang, Ali Alridha Abdulkarim, Chen Min, Zufeng Zhang, Dzmitry Tsetserukou

A ray-conditioned kernel extends each primitive with a viewing-direction subspace that… ▽ More Monocular outdoor navigation requires robust camera motion estimation and a dense, navigable map, where 3D Gaussian Splatting SLAM serves this task through an explicit differentiable parameterization and closed-form projection. However, typically, the geometry is recovered by associating 2D pixels with 3D points, so a modular SLAM system absorbs tracking error into the map, leading to cascading degradation. To address these gaps, we present SG^2SLAM, which couples streaming spatial geometry with a ray-conditioned 3DGS kernel. Streaming spatial geometry replaces the per- frame cross-dimensional correspondence with a chunk-level metric prior that supervises tracking and mapping from one source. A ray-conditioned kernel extends each primitive with a viewing-direction subspace that senses the monocular camera viewpoints. The joint optimization over spatial geometry and poses ensures a globally consistent map and trajectory outcome. Experiments on the KITTI and Waymo Open datasets show that our SG^2SLAM surpasses the per-frame association baselines in the monocular outdoor navigation task. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance. △ Less

    nucl-th

    Chebyshev-expanded covariant density functional from relativistic Brueckner theory

    Authors: Armen Sedrakian

    Abstract: We construct a density-dependent covariant energy density functional (CE-ME1) from full-Dirac-space relativistic Brueckner--Hartree--Fock (RBHF) self-energies and the corresponding equation of state. The space-like component $U_V$ is incorporated at the Fermi surface through reduced scalar and time-like self-energies, which are mapped onto local Hartree $σ$,… ▽ More We construct a density-dependent covariant energy density functional (CE-ME1) from full-Dirac-space relativistic Brueckner--Hartree--Fock (RBHF) self-energies and the corresponding equation of state. The space-like component $U_V$ is incorporated at the Fermi surface through reduced scalar and time-like self-energies, which are mapped onto local Hartree $σ$, $ω$, $δ$, and $ρ$ channels. The density-dependent couplings are represented by cubic Chebyshev polynomials over the density range $0.08\leqρ\leq0.48~{\rm fm}^{-3}$. A constrained thermodynamic calibration uses 199 RBHF energy-per-particle values from published microscopic computations at six isospin asymmetries, while maintaining positive, monotonic couplings. The Bonn-A RBHF saturation point, incompressibility $K_\infty=258$ MeV, symmetry energy $J=33.1$ MeV, and slope $L=65.2$ MeV are reproduced. The predicted curvature $K_{\rm sym}=33$ MeV differs from the published RBHF value of $-67$ MeV, illustrating the derivative sensitivity of the local reduction. The combined energy RMS is $0.613$ MeV, measuring model discrepancy rather than statistical uncertainty. Uniform matter remains mechanically stable and causal above saturation within the calibrated range. In cold charge-neutral $npeμ$ matter, CE-ME1 reasonably reproduces the interpolated RBHF pressure and proton fraction over most of the interval, although the pressure discrepancy increases near its upper edge. The DD-ME2 benchmark is stiffer and predicts a lower proton fraction at high density. △ Less

We first describe the decomposition into parabolic double cosets and compute the required stabilizers. Over a non-Archimedean field we use character obstructions, localization, and transpose symmetry. Over the real field we use a Casimir identity and explicit normal vectors to prove the corresponding statement for distributions with prescribed infinitesimal character. The Gelfand--Kazhdan criterion and a dualizing involution then give the multiplicity bound. △ Less

    q-bio.NC

    Temporal smoothness constraints on efficient neurobiological codes imply temporal specialization

    Authors: John J. Vastola, Samuel J. Gershman

    Abstract: …slowly varying temporal features, we find surprising structure of mathematical interest: optimal codes have subsystems which represent orthogonal temporal features, and which correspond to the eigenfunctions of a Sturm-Liouville problem. ▽ More Neurobiological circuits may be energy efficient in part because there is a division of labor: different subsystems compute or represent different things. In light of this, it is interesting that a major difference between subsystems is the time scale on which they typically vary. Here, we argue that temporal smoothness constraints---the idea that it may be energetically costly for systems to vary much more quickly than their typical time scale---imply a certain division of labor with respect to temporal stimulus features. In particular, slow subsystems ought to represent slowly varying stimulus features, and fast subsystems ought to represent quickly varying features. We formulate a novel efficient coding model that we use to investigate this claim, and exactly solve for the optimal division of labor. In addition to finding that slower subsystems ought to represent more slowly varying temporal features, we find surprising structure of mathematical interest: optimal codes have subsystems which represent orthogonal temporal features, and which correspond to the eigenfunctions of a Sturm-Liouville problem. △ Less

    math.OC

    Proximal Point Newton Methods for Nonconvex Optimization

    Authors: Chang He, Shuzhong Zhang

    Abstract: Tensor methods offer improved complexity guarantees for nonconvex optimization, but evaluating high-order derivatives and solving the corresponding subproblems can be computationally expensive. In this paper, we develop a proximal point Newton framework that exploits third-derivative smoothness using only gradients and Hessians. Under Hessian Lipschitz conti… ▽ More Tensor methods offer improved complexity guarantees for nonconvex optimization, but evaluating high-order derivatives and solving the corresponding subproblems can be computationally expensive. In this paper, we develop a proximal point Newton framework that exploits third-derivative smoothness using only gradients and Hessians. Under Hessian Lipschitz continuity, a single-loop proximal point Newton method that alternates between negative-curvature directions and Newton steps finds an $(\varepsilon_g,\varepsilon_H)$-approximate second-order stationary point within $\mathcal{O}(\max\{\varepsilon_g^{-3/2},\varepsilon_H^{-3}\})$ second-order oracle evaluations. When the third derivative is also Lipschitz continuous, we introduce an accelerated proximal point Newton method and obtain a dimension-free complexity of $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\max\{\varepsilon_g^{-4/3},\varepsilon_H^{-2}\})$, matching that of the third-order tensor methods up to logarithmic factors without constructing tensors or solving tensor subproblems. We further develop an adaptive inexact Newton--CG variant that requires no prior knowledge of Lipschitz constants and preserves the improved oracle complexity with high probability. Under uniformly bounded queried Hessians, this variant achieves a Hessian-vector product complexity of $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\max\{\varepsilon_g^{-5/3},\varepsilon_H^{-5/2}\})$, improving upon the previous $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\max\{\varepsilon_g^{-3}\varepsilon_H^{5/2},\varepsilon_H^{-7/2}\})$ result attained by existing second-order methods such as cubic regularized Newton method and Newton--CG method. Preliminary numerical experiments are encouraging. △ Less

    cs.CV cs.MM

    Hierarchical Multimodal Interaction Reasoning for Dyadic Relationship Recognition

    Authors: Wang Tang, Fethiye Irmak Dogan, Linbo Qing, Ce Zhu, Hatice Gunes

    Abstract: …objective, overlooking that relationships may rely on different interaction patterns. For instance, distant relationships can often be inferred from direct behavioural correspondence, whereas close relationships are better reflected by coordinated affective, behavioural, and multimodal responses. To address this limitation, we propose HiRe-DRR, a Hierarchica… ▽ More Dyadic relationship recognition aims to identify the latent social relationship between two individuals from their multimodal interactions. Existing methods typically project heterogeneous cues into a shared representation and optimise a flat classification objective, overlooking that relationships may rely on different interaction patterns. For instance, distant relationships can often be inferred from direct behavioural correspondence, whereas close relationships are better reflected by coordinated affective, behavioural, and multimodal responses. To address this limitation, we propose HiRe-DRR, a Hierarchical Multimodal Interaction Reasoning framework that organises relationship evidence from three complementary perspectives: feature level, interaction type, and relationship hierarchy. HiRe-DRR decomposes facial, bodily, acoustic, and textual cues into low- and high-level representations and constructs homogeneous and heterogeneous cross-participant interactions. We further introduce the Mixture-of-Interaction Transformer (MoI-Former), which treats diverse interactions as specialised experts and employs relation-aware sparse routing to identify relationship-relevant evidence while preserving contextual interaction information. The proposed Hierarchical Conditional Loss (HC-Loss) exploits the hierarchical nature of social relationships through coarse-to-fine supervision, enabling structured relationship reasoning. Extensive experiments on three dyadic relationship benchmarks demonstrate that HiRe-DRR outperforms recent representative image-, video-, and multimodal methods. Our routing analysis further reveals that distant relationships tend to rely on low-level homogeneous interactions, whereas closer relationships focus on high-level heterogeneous interactions. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/tw-repository/HiRe-DRR. △ Less

    astro-ph.GA

    Impact of Gaia-Sausage-Enceladus Debris on Milky Way Escape-velocity Estimates in TNG50

    Authors: Yin Wu, Haining Li, Shi Shao, Xiang-Xiang Xue, Lan Zhang, Gang Zhao

    Abstract: …underestimation of the escape velocity corresponds to an $\sim 6\%$ underestimation of the total mass. This improvement persists in LAMOST-like magnitude and footprint selection. GSE-like debris is therefore a key contributor to the systematic underestimation of escape-velocity measurements, with direct implications for the inferred MW mass. ▽ More The escape velocity is a key probe of the Milky Way (MW) potential, but standard estimates can be biased by unrelaxed substructures. Using MW analogs from the TNG50 cosmological simulation, we investigate the impact of the dominant, highly radial Gaia-Sausage-Enceladus (GSE) debris on escape-velocity measurements. We introduce an adaptive selection strategy that balances statistical reliability with sample size to identify the power-law decline regime of the local halo velocity distribution. Applying this strategy to 16 MW analogs with prominent GSE-like structures and continuous high-velocity tails, we find that retaining GSE-like stars yields a systematic underestimate, with a recovery fraction of $97.5^{+2.1}_{-2.9}\%$, while removing GSE-like stars alone increases the recovery fraction to $99.4^{+2.1}_{-2.5}\%$. The $\sim 2\%$ underestimation of the escape velocity corresponds to an $\sim 6\%$ underestimation of the total mass. This improvement persists in LAMOST-like magnitude and footprint selection. GSE-like debris is therefore a key contributor to the systematic underestimation of escape-velocity measurements, with direct implications for the inferred MW mass. △ Less

    econ.TH

    Optimally Selling Heterogeneous Objects by Quantity

    Authors: Xun Chen

    Abstract: …ex ante symmetry, assuming free disposal, independence between the quantity-value vector and the vector of bundle shortfalls from their respective quantity values, and corresponding distributional and support conditions. For an arbitrary number of objects, applications yield explicit optimal menus that involve quantity discounts, sparse quantity options, or… ▽ More I study the optimality of selling K heterogeneous objects to a buyer with private values through a quantity menu: the seller posts a price for each number of objects, and the buyer chooses a quantity, pays its price, and selects any objects up to that quantity. I propose a pricing method that aggregates critical values for second differences of quantity values (the highest values attainable at each quantity). Critical values can be determined separately when each second difference's conditional inverse-hazard ratio depends only on that difference. For additive values, I show that the resulting menu is optimal among all incentive-compatible and individually rational mechanisms under explicit distributional and support conditions, including ex ante symmetry across object labels. A separate result applies the same pricing method to obtain an optimal menu for nonadditive bundle values without ex ante symmetry, assuming free disposal, independence between the quantity-value vector and the vector of bundle shortfalls from their respective quantity values, and corresponding distributional and support conditions. For an arbitrary number of objects, applications yield explicit optimal menus that involve quantity discounts, sparse quantity options, or two-part tariffs. These results differ from those establishing the optimality of pure bundling in Haghpanah and Hartline (2021), nested bundling in Yang (2025), separate sales in Kwak (2026), and core-peripheral bundling in Zhong (2026). △ Less
